```text
100	  \mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}^2]
101	  &= \mathbb{E}[T^2] - \textstyle\sum_j q_j\bigl(\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j\bigr)^2\\
102	  &= s_T - \textstyle\sum_j \mathrm{ct}_j^2/q_j,\\
103	  \mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}\,Y]
104	  &= \mathbb{E}[TY] - \textstyle\sum_j \bigl(\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j\bigr)\,\mathbb{E}[Y\cdot D_j^*]\\
105	  &= \mu_{TY} - \textstyle\sum_j \mathrm{ct}_j\,\mathrm{cy}_j/q_j.
106	\end{align*}
107	\end{proof}
108	
109	\paragraph{Verification at $C=I$.}
110	When $C=I$: $q_j=p_j$, $\mathrm{ct}_j=\mathbb{E}[T D_j]=\alpha_j p_j$, $\mathrm{cy}_j=\mathbb{E}[Y D_j]=p_j(\tau\alpha_j+\beta_j^{(A)})$.
111	Direct substitution yields numerator $= \tau\sigma_T^2$ and denominator $= \sigma_T^2$, giving $\operatorname{plim}\hat\tau = \tau$.
112	
113	\paragraph{Bias structure.}
114	By Lemma~\ref{lem:bias_decomp}:
115	\begin{align}
116	  \operatorname{plim}\hat\tau(C) &= \tau + B(C),\notag\\
117	  B(C) &= \frac{\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),\,f(A)\mid A^*)]}{D(C)}.
118	  \label{eq:bias_conf}
119	\end{align}
120	By the law of total covariance:
121	\begin{multline}
122	  \mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Cov}(g,f\mid A^*)]
123	  = \operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))\\
124	  - \operatorname{Cov}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[g(A)\mid A^*],\;\mathbb{E}[f(A)\mid A^*]\bigr).
125	  \label{eq:lotc}
126	\end{multline}
127	
128	At $C=I$: $\mathbb{E}[g\mid A^*]=g(A)$, so the between-group covariance equals $\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)$ and $B(I)=0$.
129	
130	At $C=\frac{1}{K}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^\top$ (uniform, $A^*\perp A$): $\mathbb{E}[g\mid A^*]$ is constant, so the between-group covariance is zero and
131	\begin{equation}
132	  B_{\mathrm{OV}} \;\equiv\; B\bigl(\tfrac{1}{K}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^\top\bigr)
133	  = \frac{\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),\,f(A))}{\operatorname{Var}(T)},
134	  \label{eq:bov}
135	\end{equation}
136	which equals the classical omitted-variable bias (no control for $A$).
137	
138	\paragraph{Sign preservation condition.}
139	Since $D(C)>0$, sign$(\operatorname{plim}\hat\tau) = $ sign$(\tau\cdot D(C) + R(C))$.
140	For sign preservation, it suffices that $R(C)$ does not overwhelm $\tau\cdot D(C)$.
141	
142	\begin{proposition}[Confounding sign preservation --- proportional case]
143	\label{prop:conf_prop}
144	If $f(A) = \lambda\,g(A)$ for some constant $\lambda$ (i.e., the confounder effects on $T$ and $Y$ are proportional), then
145	\begin{equation}
146	  B(C) = \bigl(1-\xi(C)\bigr)\,B_{\mathrm{OV}},
147	  \label{eq:convex}
148	\end{equation}
149	where
150	\[
151	  \xi(C) = \frac{V_B\;\sigma_T^2}
152	  {V\bigl(V-V_B+\sigma_T^2\bigr)}
153	  \;\in\;[0,1],
154	\]
155	where $V_B=\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g(A)\mid A^*])$ and $V=\operatorname{Var}(g(A))$.
156	Consequently, $B(C)$ lies between $0$ and $B_{\mathrm{OV}}$, and $\operatorname{sign}(\operatorname{plim}\hat\tau)=\operatorname{sign}(\tau)$ whenever $\operatorname{sign}(\tau)=\operatorname{sign}(\tau+B_{\mathrm{OV}})$.
157	\end{proposition}
158	
159	\begin{proof}
160	When $f=\lambda g$: the between-group covariance in~\eqref{eq:lotc} becomes $\lambda\,\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g\mid A^*])$, and $\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)=\lambda\,\operatorname{Var}(g)$.
161	So $R(C)=\lambda\bigl(\operatorname{Var}(g)-\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g\mid A^*])\bigr)=\lambda\,V_W$, where $V_W=\operatorname{Var}(g)-V_B$ with $V_B=\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g\mid A^*])$.
162	Similarly, $D(C)=V_W+\sigma_T^2$.
163	Hence
164	\[
165	  B(C) = \frac{\lambda\,V_W}{V_W+\sigma_T^2},\qquad
166	  B_{\mathrm{OV}} = \frac{\lambda\,V}{V+\sigma_T^2},
167	\]
168	where $V=\operatorname{Var}(g)=V_B+V_W$.
169	The ratio
170	\[
171	  \frac{B(C)}{B_{\mathrm{OV}}} = \frac{V_W(V+\sigma_T^2)}{V(V_W+\sigma_T^2)} = 1 - \xi(C)
172	\]
173	is a decreasing function of $V_B\in[0,V]$.
174	At $V_B=0$ (no classification, $C$ uniform): $\xi=0$, $B=B_{\mathrm{OV}}$.
175	At $V_B=V$ (perfect classification, $C=I$): $\xi=1$, $B=0$.
176	Since $0\leq V_B\leq V$, we have $\xi\in[0,1]$ and $B(C)\in[0,B_{\mathrm{OV}}]$ (with the sign of $B_{\mathrm{OV}}$).
177	\end{proof}
178	
179	\begin{proposition}[Confounding sign preservation --- general case]
180	\label{prop:conf_gen}
181	For general $g$ and $f$ (not necessarily proportional), define the worst-case bias bound
182	\begin{equation}
183	  \bar{B} \;=\; \frac{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\,\operatorname{Var}(f(A))}}{\sigma_T^2}.
184	  \label{eq:bbar}
185	\end{equation}
186	If $|\tau| > \bar{B}$, then $\operatorname{sign}(\operatorname{plim}\hat\tau(C))=\operatorname{sign}(\tau)$ for all column-stochastic $C$.
187	\end{proposition}
188	
189	\begin{proof}
190	We need $\operatorname{sign}(\tau\cdot D(C)+R(C))=\operatorname{sign}(\tau)$, which holds if $|R(C)|<|\tau|\cdot D(C)$.
191	Since $D(C)\geq\sigma_T^2$ (shown in Lemma~\ref{lem:bias_decomp}), it suffices to show $|R(C)|<|\tau|\,\sigma_T^2$.
192	
193	\emph{Step 1: Bound $|R(C)|$.}
194	$R(C)=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A)\mid A^*)]$.
195	By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to each within-group covariance:
196	\begin{multline*}
197	  |\operatorname{Cov}(g,f\mid A^*{=}j)|\\
198	  \leq \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g\mid A^*{=}j)\,\operatorname{Var}(f\mid A^*{=}j)}.
199	\end{multline*}
200	Taking expectations and applying Cauchy--Schwarz again:
201	\begin{align*}
202	  |R(C)|
203	  &\leq \mathbb{E}\bigl[\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g\mid A^*)\,\operatorname{Var}(f\mid A^*)}\bigr]\\
204	  &\leq \sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(g\mid A^*)]\;\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(f\mid A^*)]}\\
205	  &\leq \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\;\operatorname{Var}(f(A))},
206	\end{align*}
207	where the last step uses $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(X\mid Y)]\leq\operatorname{Var}(X)$ (law of total variance).
208	
209	\emph{Step 2: Conclude.}
210	The bias satisfies
211	\[
212	  |B(C)| = \frac{|R(C)|}{D(C)} \leq \frac{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g)\,\operatorname{Var}(f)}}{\sigma_T^2} = \bar{B}.
213	\]
214	When $|\tau|>\bar{B}$, the bias cannot cancel $\tau$, so $\operatorname{sign}(\operatorname{plim}\hat\tau)=\operatorname{sign}(\tau)$ for all $C$.
215	\end{proof}
216	
217	\paragraph{Relationship to the omitted-variable condition.}
218	A weaker (necessary) condition for sign preservation is $\operatorname{sign}(\tau)=\operatorname{sign}(\tau_{\mathrm{OV}})$ where $\tau_{\mathrm{OV}}=\tau+\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)/\operatorname{Var}(T)$.
219	If this fails, then $C=\frac{1}{K}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^\top$ (uniform noise) already flips the sign.
220	The rigorous sufficient condition $|\tau|>\bar{B}$ implies the weaker condition, since $|B_{\mathrm{OV}}|=|\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)|/\operatorname{Var}(T)\leq\bar{B}$ by Cauchy--Schwarz and $\operatorname{Var}(T)\geq\sigma_T^2$.
221	
222	\paragraph{Verification for paper parameters ($K{=}3$).}
223	With $\tau{=}2.0$, $\boldsymbol{\alpha}{=}(1.5,{-}1.0)$, $\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(A)}{=}(1.0,{-}0.5)$, $\sigma_T{=}1.0$, $\mathbf{p}{=}(0.40,0.35,0.25)$:
224	\begin{align*}
225	  \operatorname{Var}(g) &= \textstyle\sum_k p_k\alpha_k^2 - (\sum_k p_k\alpha_k)^2\\
226	  &= 0.962,\\
227	  \operatorname{Var}(f) &= \textstyle\sum_k p_k(\beta_k^{(A)})^2 - (\sum_k p_k\beta_k^{(A)})^2\\
228	  &= 0.362,\\
229	  \bar{B} &= \sqrt{0.962\times 0.362}/1.0^2 = 0.590.
230	\end{align*}
231	Since $|\tau|{=}2.0 > \bar{B}{=}0.590$, sign preservation is guaranteed for all column-stochastic $C$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:conf_gen}.
232	Moreover, $B_{\mathrm{OV}}=\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)/\operatorname{Var}(T)=0.588/1.962={+}0.300$, giving $\tau_{\mathrm{OV}}{=}2.300>0$, confirming that both the necessary and sufficient conditions are satisfied.
233	
234	\paragraph{Extension to $K > 3$.}
235	For $K{>}3$, treatment effect coefficients are drawn from a fixed pool with alternating signs and decreasing magnitudes:
236	\begin{multline*}
237	  \boldsymbol{\beta} = (0,\; 1.0,\; {-}0.5,\; 0.7,\; {-}0.3,\\
238	  0.5,\; {-}0.2,\; 0.4,\; {-}0.15,\; 0.3,\; \ldots),
239	\end{multline*}
240	truncated to $K$ entries. The category distribution uses $p_k \propto 1/(k{+}1)$, normalized to sum to~1. Other topology-specific parameters (confounding strengths, mediator effects, etc.)\ follow analogous alternating-sign pools.
241	
242	\subsection{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone} (Confounding Monotonicity)}
243	\label{app:monotonicity_proof}
244	
245	Under the interpolation $C(\delta)=(1{-}\delta)I+\delta C_0$, we prove $|B(C(\delta))|$ is non-decreasing in $\delta$ for proportional effects ($f=\lambda g$) and provide numerical verification for the general case.
246	The proof proceeds by direct algebraic analysis of $V_B(\delta)=\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g(A)\mid A^*_\delta])$ as a rational function of $\delta$, establishing convexity and bounding the derivative at both endpoints.
247	
248	\paragraph{Proportional case ($f=\lambda g$).}
249	From Proposition~\ref{prop:conf_prop}, $B(C)=\lambda V_W/(V_W+\sigma_T^2)$ where $V_W=\operatorname{Var}(g)-V_B$ and $V_B(\delta)=\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g(A)\mid A^*_\delta])$.
250	
251	\emph{Step 1: Convexity of $V_B(\delta)$.}
252	Define $N_j(\delta)=\sum_k g_k\,C(\delta)_{jk}\,p_k$ and $q_j(\delta)=\sum_k C(\delta)_{jk}\,p_k$.
253	Both are affine in $\delta$, with $q_j(\delta)>0$.
254	Then $V_B(\delta)=\sum_j N_j(\delta)^2/q_j(\delta) - \bar{g}^2$.
255	Each term $N_j^2/q_j$ is the composition of the perspective function $(x,t)\mapsto x^2/t$ (convex for $t>0$) with the affine map $\delta\mapsto(N_j(\delta),\,q_j(\delta))$, hence convex in $\delta$.
256	A sum of convex functions is convex, so $V_B(\delta)$ is convex on $[0,\delta_{\max}]$.
257	
258	\emph{Step 2: Derivative formula.}
259	Let $\mu_j(\delta)=N_j(\delta)/q_j(\delta)=\mathbb{E}[g(A)\mid A^*{=}j]$.
260	Then
261	\begin{equation}
262	\frac{dV_B}{d\delta}
263	= -\sum_{j,k}\bigl[(C_0)_{jk}-\mathbf{1}_{j=k}\bigr]\,p_k\,(g_k-\mu_j(\delta))^2.
264	\label{eq:dVB}
265	\end{equation}
266	\emph{Derivation.}
267	$\frac{d}{d\delta}\frac{N_j^2}{q_j}=2\mu_j\dot{N}_j-\mu_j^2\dot{q}_j$
268	where $\dot{N}_j=m_j^0-g_jp_j$ and $\dot{q}_j=q_j^0-p_j$ (with $m_j^0=\sum_k g_k(C_0)_{jk}p_k$, $q_j^0=\sum_k(C_0)_{jk}p_k$).
269	Substituting and using column-stochasticity ($\sum_j(C_0)_{jk}=\sum_j\mathbf{1}_{j=k}=1$) to cancel the $g_k^2$ terms yields~\eqref{eq:dVB}.
270	
271	\emph{Step 3: $V_B'(0)\leq 0$ (universal, no conditions on $C_0$).}
272	At $\delta{=}0$: $\mu_j(0)=g_j$ (since $C(0)=I$), so $(g_k-\mu_j(0))^2=(g_k-g_j)^2$.
273	The diagonal terms ($j{=}k$) vanish.
274	Hence
275	\[
276	V_B'(0)=-\sum_{j\neq k}(C_0)_{jk}\,p_k\,(g_j-g_k)^2\;\leq\; 0,
277	\]
278	since every term is non-negative.
279	
280	\emph{Step 4: $V_B'(1)\leq 0$ for $K{=}2$.}
281	For $K{=}2$ with $C_0=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}a&1{-}b\\1{-}a&b\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]$ and $a,b\geq\tfrac{1}{2}$,
282	set $g_0{=}0$, $g_1{=}1$ without loss of generality
283	(the general case scales by $(\Delta g)^2$).
284	At $\delta{=}1$: $q_0{=}ap_0{+}(1{-}b)p_1$, $q_1{=}(1{-}a)p_0{+}bp_1$,
285	$\mu_0{=}(1{-}b)p_1/q_0$, $\mu_1{=}bp_1/q_1$.
286	Expanding~\eqref{eq:dVB} at $\delta{=}1$ and factoring:
287	\begin{align*}
288	  V_B'(1) &= (1{-}a)\,p_0(\mu_0^2{-}\mu_1^2)\\
289	  &\quad{}+(1{-}b)\,p_1\bigl[(1{-}\mu_1)^2{-}(1{-}\mu_0)^2\bigr] \\
290	  &= (\mu_0{-}\mu_1)\,\bigl[(1{-}a)\,p_0(\mu_0{+}\mu_1)\\
291	  &\quad{}+(1{-}b)\,p_1(2{-}\mu_0{-}\mu_1)\bigr].
292	\end{align*}
293	The bracket is non-negative (since $0\leq\mu_j\leq 1$ and all coefficients are non-negative).
294	Direct calculation gives $\mu_0{-}\mu_1=p_0\,p_1(1{-}a{-}b)/(q_0\,q_1)$
295	(expand $(1{-}b)\,q_1-b\,q_0=p_0(1{-}a{-}b)$).
296	Under $a,b\geq\tfrac{1}{2}$: $a{+}b\geq 1$, so $\mu_0{-}\mu_1\leq 0$ and $V_B'(1)\leq 0$.
297	
298	\emph{Step 5: Monotonicity from convexity.}
299	Since $V_B$ is convex (Step~1), its derivative $V_B'$ is non-decreasing.
```

classical omitted-variable bias (no control for $A$).\n\n\\paragraph{Sign preservation condition.}\nSince $D(C)>0$, sign$(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau) = $ sign$(\\tau\\cdot D(C) + R(C))$.\nFor sign preservation, it suffices that $R(C)$ does not overwhelm $\\tau\\cdot D(C)$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Confounding sign preservation --- proportional case]\n\\label{prop:conf_prop}\nIf $f(A) = \\lambda\\,g(A)$ for some constant $\\lambda$ (i.e., the confounder effects on $T$ and $Y$ are proportional), then\n\\begin{equation}\n  B(C) = \\bigl(1-\\xi(C)\\bigr)\\,B_{\\mathrm{OV}},\n  \\label{eq:convex}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere\n\\[\n  \\xi(C) = \\frac{V_B\\;\\sigma_T^2}\n  {V\\bigl(V-V_B+\\sigma_T^2\\bigr)}\n  \\;\\in\\;[0,1],\n\\]\nwhere $V_B=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*])$ and $V=\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))$.\nConsequently, $B(C)$ lies between $0$ and $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$, and $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ whenever $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau+B_{\\mathrm{OV}})$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWhen $f=\\lambda g$: the between-group covariance in~\\eqref{eq:lotc} becomes $\\lambda\\,\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])$, and $\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)=\\lambda\\,\\operatorname{Var}(g)$.\nSo $R(C)=\\lambda\\bigl(\\operatorname{Var}(g)-\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])\\bigr)=\\lambda\\,V_W$, where $V_W=\\operatorname{Var}(g)-V_B$ with $V_B=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])$.\nSimilarly, $D(C)=V_W+\\sigma_T^2$.\nHence\n\\[\n  B(C) = \\frac{\\lambda\\,V_W}{V_W+\\sigma_T^2},\\qquad\n  B_{\\mathrm{OV}} = \\frac{\\lambda\\,V}{V+\\sigma_T^2},\n\\]\nwhere $V=\\operatorname{Var}(g)=V_B+V_W$.\nThe ratio\n\\[\n  \\frac{B(C)}{B_{\\mathrm{OV}}} = \\frac{V_W(V+\\sigma_T^2)}{V(V_W+\\sigma_T^2)} = 1 - \\xi(C)\n\\]\nis a decreasing function of $V_B\\in[0,V]$.\nAt $V_B=0$ (no classification, $C$ uniform): $\\xi=0$, $B=B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$.\nAt $V_B=V$ (perfect classification, $C=I$): $\\xi=1$, $B=0$.\nSince $0\\leq V_B\\leq V$, we have $\\xi\\in[0,1]$ and $B(C)\\in[0,B_{\\mathrm{OV}}]$ (with the sign of $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$).\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Confounding sign preservation --- general case]\n\\label{prop:conf_gen}\nFor general $g$ and $f$ (not necessarily proportional), define the worst-case bias bound\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\bar{B} \\;=\\; \\frac{\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f(A))}}{\\sigma_T^2}.\n  \\label{eq:bbar}\n\\end{equation}\nIf $|\\tau| > \\bar{B}$, then $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C))=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ for all column-stochastic $C$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWe need $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau\\cdot D(C)+R(C))=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$, which holds if $|R(C)|<|\\tau|\\cdot D(C)$.\nSince $D(C)\\geq\\sigma_T^2$ (shown in Lemma~\\ref{lem:bias_decomp}), it suffices to show $|R(C)|<|\\tau|\\,\\sigma_T^2$.\n\n\\emph{Step 1: Bound $|R(C)|$.}\n$R(C)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A)\\mid A^*)]$.\nBy the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to each within-group covariance:\n\\begin{multline*}\n  |\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f\\mid A^*{=}j)|\\\\\n  \\leq \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*{=}j)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*{=}j)}.\n\\end{multline*}\nTaking expectations and applying Cauchy--Schwarz again:\n\\begin{align*}\n  |R(C)|\n  &\\leq \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*)}\\bigr]\\\\\n  &\\leq \\sqrt{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*)]\\;\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*)]}\\\\\n  &\\leq \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\;\\operatorname{Var}(f(A))},\n\\end{align*}\nwhere the last step uses $\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(X\\mid Y)]\\leq\\operatorname{Var}(X)$ (law of total variance).\n\n\\emph{Step 2: Conclude.}\nThe bias satisfies\n\\[\n  |B(C)| = \\frac{|R(C)|}{D(C)} \\leq \\frac{\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f)}}{\\sigma_T^2} = \\bar{B}.\n\\]\nWhen $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}$, the bias cannot cancel $\\tau$, so $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ for all $C$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to the omitted-variable condition.}\nA weaker (necessary) condition for sign preservation is $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}})$ where $\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}}=\\tau+\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$.\nIf this fails, then $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$ (uniform noise) already flips the sign.\nThe rigorous sufficient condition $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}$ implies the weaker condition, since $|B_{\\mathrm{OV}}|=|\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)|/\\operatorname{Var}(T)\\leq\\bar{B}$ by Cauchy--Schwarz and $\\operatorname{Var}(T)\\geq\\sigma_T^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Verification for paper parameters ($K{=}3$).}\nWith $\\tau{=}2.0$, $\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}{=}(1.5,{-}1.0)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}^{(A)}{=}(1.0,{-}0.5)$, $\\sigma_T{=}1.0$, $\\mathbf{p}{=}(0.40,0.35,0.25)$:\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\operatorname{Var}(g) &= \\textstyle\\sum_k p_k\\alpha_k^2 - (\\sum_k p_k\\alpha_k)^2\\\\\n  &= 0.962,\\\\\n  \\operatorname{Var}(f) &= \\textstyle\\sum_k p_k(\\beta_k^{(A)})^2 - (\\sum_k p_k\\beta_k^{(A)})^2\\\\\n  &= 0.362,\\\\\n  \\bar{B} &= \\sqrt{0.962\\times 0.362}/1.0^2 = 0.590.\n\\end{align*}\nSince $|\\tau|{=}2.0 > \\bar{B}{=}0.590$, sign preservation is guaranteed for all column-stochastic $C$ by Proposition~\\ref{prop:conf_gen}.\nMoreover, $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}=\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)/\\operatorname{Var}(T)=0.588/1.962={+}0.300$, giving $\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}}{=}2.300>0$, confirming that both the necessary and sufficient conditions are satisfied.\n\n\\paragraph{Extension to $K > 3$.}\nFor $K{>}3$, treatment effect coefficients are drawn from a fixed pool with alternating signs and decreasing magnitudes:\n\\begin{multline*}\n  \\boldsymbol{\\beta} = (0,\\; 1.0,\\; {-}0.5,\\; 0.7,\\; {-}0.3,\\\\\n  0.5,\\; {-}0.2,\\; 0.4,\\; {-}0.15,\\; 0.3,\\; \\ldots),\n\\end{multline*}\ntruncated to $K$ entries. The category distribution uses $p_k \\propto 1/(k{+}1)$, normalized to sum to~1. Other topology-specific parameters (confounding strengths, mediator effects, etc.)\\ follow analogous alternating-sign pools.\n\n\\subsection{Proof of Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} (Confounding Monotonicity)}\n\\label{app:monotonicity_proof}\n\nUnder the interpolation $C(\\delta)=(1{-}\\delta)I+\\delta C_0$, we prove $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is non-decreasing in $\\delta$ for proportional effects ($f=\\lambda g$) and provide numerical verification for the general case.\nThe proof proceeds by direct algebraic analysis of $V_B(\\delta)=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*_\\delta])$ as a rational function of $\\delta$, establishing convexity and bounding the derivative at both endpoints.\n\n\\paragraph{Proportional case ($f=\\lambda g$).}\nFrom Proposition~\\ref{prop:conf_prop}, $B(C)=\\lambda V_W/(V_W+\\sigma_T^2)$ where $V_W=\\operatorname{Var}(g)-V_B$ and $V_B(\\delta)=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*_\\delta])$.\n\n\\emph{Step 1: Convexity of $V_B(\\delta)$.}\nDefine $N_j(\\delta)=\\sum_k g_k\\,C(\\delta)_{jk}\\,p_k$ and $q_j(\\delta)=\\sum_k C(\\delta)_{jk}\\,p_k$.\nBoth are affine in $\\delta$, with $q_j(\\delta)>0$.\nThen $V_B(\\delta)=\\sum_j N_j(\\delta)^2/q_j(\\delta) - \\bar{g}^2$.\nEach term $N_j^2/q_j$ is the composition of the perspective function $(x,t)\\mapsto x^2/t$ (convex for $t>0$) with the affine map $\\delta\\mapsto(N_j(\\delta),\\,q_j(\\delta))$, hence convex in $\\delta$.\nA sum of convex functions is convex, so $V_B(\\delta)$ is convex on $[0,\\delta_{\\max}]$.\n\n\\emph{Step 2: Derivative formula.}\nLet $\\mu_j(\\delta)=N_j(\\delta)/q_j(\\delta)=\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*{=}j]$.\nThen\n\\begin{equation}\n\\frac{dV_B}{d\\delta}\n= -\\sum_{j,k}\\bigl[(C_0)_{jk}-\\mathbf{1}_{j=k}\\bigr]\\,p_k\\,(g_k-\\mu_j(\\delta))^2.\n\\label{eq:dVB}\n\\end{equation}\n\\emph{Derivation.}\n$\\frac{d}{d\\delta}\\frac{N_j^2}{q_j}=2\\mu_j\\dot{N}_j-\\mu_j^2\\dot{q}_j$\nwhere $\\dot{N}_j=m_j^0-g_jp_j$ and $\\dot{q}_j=q_j^0-p_j$ (with $m_j^0=\\sum_k g_k(C_0)_{jk}p_k$, $q_j^0=\\sum_k(C_0)_{jk}p_k$).\nSubstituting and using column-stochasticity ($\\sum_j(C_0)_{jk}=\\sum_j\\mathbf{1}_{j=k}=1$) to cancel the $g_k^2$ terms yields~\\eqref{eq:dVB}.\n\n\\emph{Step 3: $V_B'(0)\\leq 0$ (universal, no conditions on $C_0$).}\nAt $\\delta{=}0$: $\\mu_j(0)=g_j$ (since $C(0)=I$), so $(g_k-\\mu_j(0))^2=(g_k-g_j)^2$.\nThe diagonal terms ($j{=}k$) vanish.\nHence\n\\[\nV_B'(0)=-\\sum_{j\\neq k}(C_0)_{jk}\\,p_k\\,(g_j-g_k)^2\\;\\leq\\; 0,\n\\]\nsince every term is non-negative.\n\n\\emph{Step 4: $V_B'(1)\\leq 0$ for $K{=}2$.}\nFor $K{=}2$ with $C_0=\\bigl[\\begin{smallmatrix}a&1{-}b\\\\1{-}a&b\\end{smallmatrix}\\bigr]$ and (1{-}a)\\,p_0(\\mu_0^2{-}\\mu_1^2)\\\\\n  &\\quad{}+(1{-}b)\\,p_1\\bigl[(1{-}\\mu_1)^2{-}(1{-}\\mu_0)^2\\bigr] \\\\\n  &= (\\mu_0{-}\\mu_1)\\,\\bigl[(1{-}a)\\,p_0(\\mu_0{+}\\mu_1)\\\\\n  &\\quad{}+(1{-}b)\\,p_1(2{-}\\mu_0{-}\\mu_1)\\bigr].\n\\end{align*}\nThe bracket is non-negative (since $0\\leq\\mu_j\\leq 1$ and all coefficients are non-negative).\nDirect calculation gives $\\mu_0{-}\\mu_1=p_0\\,p_1(1{-}a{-}b)/(q_0\\,q_1)$\n(expand $(1{-}b)\\,q_1-b\\,q_0=p_0(1{-}a{-}b)$).\nUnder $a,b\\geq\\tfrac{1}{2}$: $a{+}b\\geq 1$, so $\\mu_0{-}\\mu_1\\leq 0$ and $V_B'(1)\\leq 0$.\n\n\\emph{Step 5: Monotonicity from convexity.}\nSince $V_B$ is convex (Step~1), its derivative $V_B'$ is non-decreasing.",

969	\subsection{Applied Illustration: ClinicalBERT Case Study}
970	\label{app:case_study}
971	
972	To demonstrate the framework's end-to-end utility, we apply it retrospectively to \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024}, who use ClinicalBERT to classify patient smoking status ($K{=}4$: never, past, current, unknown) from clinical notes and control for this predicted confounder when estimating the causal effect of echocardiography on mortality in 4{,}735 MIMIC-III sepsis patients.
973	Our taxonomy identifies this as a confounding topology: the annotated variable enters as a confounder between treatment and outcome.
974	Three conclusions follow directly.
975	First, the zero sign-flip guarantee for confounding (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}) ensures that the estimated protective effect (OR~$= 0.89$) cannot reverse direction due to annotation error, a reassurance unavailable without topology-specific analysis.
976	Second, the FWL invariance result implies that any correction reducing to $C^{-1}$ applied to the dummy-coded confounder achieves exactly 0\% bias reduction for the treatment coefficient; naive plug-in is provably futile here.
977	Third, our correction guidance ranks PPI++ (83.2\% RMSE reduction for confounding) above MC-SIMEX (35.1\%) when gold labels are available, a concrete recommendation the original study lacked.
978	This example illustrates the intended workflow: DAG identification $\to$ topology-specific guarantees $\to$ correction method selection.
979	
980	\subsection{IV Wald Estimator: Diagonal-Dominance Subspaces}
981	\label{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}

53	
54	\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}
55	When $A$ mediates the $X{\to}Y$ relationship~\cite{vanderweele2015explanation}, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
56	\begin{equation}
57	\label{eq:mediation}
58	\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}(A^*) = \tau_{\text{direct}} + \bigl(1 - \lambda(C)\bigr)\,\tau_{\text{indirect}},
59	\end{equation}
60	where $\lambda(C) \in [0,1]$ under proportional effects (Appendix~\ref{app:proof}, Proposition~\ref{prop:lambda_bound}), with $\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$).
61	
62	\paragraph{Collider, M-bias, and front-door.}
63	Three topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates Berkson bias by weakening the spurious path, so annotation error is protective ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$). \textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\cite{greenland2003}, misclassifying $A$ attenuates the non-causal path, making this topology immune ($\text{ASV} = \infty$). \textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} requires two stages ($X \to A$ and $A \to Y$), each attenuated by $C$; the compound effect produces moderate bias with $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$.
64	
65	\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}
66	The Wald estimator~\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\hat{\tau}_{\text{IV}} = \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign; under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\% produce sign reversal; IV is the most fragile topology (Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}).
67

1010	
1011	\subsection{Non-Differential Misclassification Verification}
1012	\label{sec:nondiff_verify}
1013	
1014	The non-differential assumption $P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k)$ holds by design in standard LLM annotation pipelines: the model receives only source text as input and classifies without observing the outcome $Y$ or covariates $\mathbf{Z}$. The annotation API call is structurally independent of downstream variables, satisfying the conditional independence requirement.
1015	
1016	To provide empirical support beyond the design argument, we examine topic-stratified confusion matrices using the VAST dataset ($K{=}3$, stance detection), where the \texttt{topic} field (e.g., ``gun control,'' ``college'') serves as a proxy for latent factors correlated with $Y$. Across 10 model configurations (3 LLMs $\times$ 2--4 prompt types) and 19 qualifying topics (minimum 10 samples per topic), we compute per-topic confusion matrices $C_{\text{topic}}(t) = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k, \text{topic}{=}t)$ and compare against the overall $C = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k)$.
1017	
1018	Per-topic sample sizes range from 10 to 42, making individual topic CMs inherently noisy. A bootstrap permutation test ($B{=}10{,}000$) under the non-differential null shows that the expected grand maximum deviation $\max_{t,j,k} |C_{\text{topic}}(j,k) - C(j,k)|$ at the 95th percentile ranges from 0.90 to 0.98 across model configurations, driven by the smallest topics. For the 5 largest topics ($n \geq 28$), expected per-topic max deviation at the 95th percentile is 0.38--0.49, consistent with finite-sample variation rather than systematic topic-dependence. The observed per-topic variation is fully consistent with the non-differential null.
1019	
1020	This proxy-based test is necessarily limited: topic is not the actual outcome $Y$, and small per-topic samples reduce statistical power. The design argument (LLM input excludes $Y$) remains the primary justification; the stress test in Section~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress} below quantifies bias degradation under hypothetical violations.
1021	
1022	\subsection{Non-Differential Assumption Stress Test}
1023	\label{sec:nondiff_stress}
1024

1110	\subsection{Cross-$K$ Robustness}
1111	\label{sec:cross_k_robustness}
1112	
1113	All structural patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (100{,}000 matrices each): sign-flip guarantees hold for the same five topologies, and IV sign-flip rates remain within 51--53\%.
1114	
1115	\subsection{ASV Diagnostic Results}
1116	\label{sec:asv_diagnostics}
1117	
1118	For HumAID ($K{=}10$, 56 scenarios), 55.4\% are flagged as magnitude-ASV-unsafe: IV, mediation, front-door, and exposure all trigger, while confounding, collider, and M-bias remain below 10\% bias (Figure~\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). For CivilComments ($K{=}2$), 100\% of scenarios are ASV-unsafe despite $F_1$ ranging from $0.69$ (7B) to $0.82$ (frontier).
1119	
1120	Overlaying all 23 empirical matrices onto the theoretical Dirichlet violation-rate curves (Figure~\ref{fig:dirichlet_overlay}) confirms placement: VAST in the danger zone, HumAID mid-range, CivilComments approaching safe for exposure but still in IV amplification.
1121	
1122	\subsection{ASV Stability Across DGP Parameters}
1123	\label{sec:asv_stability}
1124	
1125	We test whether ASV rankings are stable across different data-generating process (DGP) configurations by varying the treatment-effect vector $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ across three settings: alternating-large ($\boldsymbol{\beta}=[0,\,1.0,\,-0.5]$), same-sign ($\boldsymbol{\beta}=[0,\,0.5,\,0.3]$), and alternating-small ($\boldsymbol{\beta}=[0,\,0.2,\,-0.1]$), with $K{=}3$, $\tau{=}0.5$, and five $C_0$ diagonal values $\in\{0.5,0.6,0.7,0.8,0.9\}$.
1126	
1127	\input{exp_e_asv_stability_table.tex}
1128	
1129	Three patterns emerge (Table~\ref{tab:asv_stability}). First, IV magnitude ASV is identical across all three DGPs ($\delta^*{=}0.203$). This is a mathematical identity, not an empirical finding: the Wald estimator $\hat{\tau}_{\text{IV}} = \text{RF}/\text{FS}$ has $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ in both numerator and denominator, and the ratio depends only on $C$ and the first-stage structure, making ASV invariant to $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ by construction. Second, exposure ASV depends on the coefficient ratio structure: alternating-large ($\beta_2/\beta_1 = -0.5$) and alternating-small ($\beta_2/\beta_1 = -0.5$) share the same ratio and yield identical $\delta^*{=}0.090$, while same-sign ($\beta_2/\beta_1 = 0.6$) gives $\delta^*{=}0.223$; thus the experiment provides two distinct sign-structure conditions, not three. Third, confounding ASV depends on effect magnitudes: only alternating-large triggers the 10\% threshold ($\delta^*{=}0.348$); smaller effects never reach 10\% bias.
```

1248	
1249	\subsection{PPI++ Gold Label Ratio Sensitivity}
1250	\label{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}
1251	
1252	We test PPI++ correction performance as a function of gold label fraction ($K{=}3$, $N{=}10{,}000$, 100 MC replications, 5 Dirichlet confusion matrices with $\min_k C_{kk}{\geq}0.6$).
1253	
1254	Negative values indicate PPI++ increases RMSE relative to the naive estimator.
1255	
1256	\begin{table}[H]
1257	\centering
1258	\caption{PPI++ RMSE reduction (\%) vs.\ gold label fraction.}
1259	\label{tab:ppi_gold_ratio}
1260	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
1261	\toprule
1262	Topology & 5\% & 10\% & 20\% & 50\% & 100\% \\
1263	\midrule
1264	Confounding & $+$84.8 & $+$88.6 & $+$91.8 & $+$94.2 & $+$95.2 \\
1265	Exposure & $+$75.2 & $+$83.1 & $+$87.1 & $+$91.2 & $+$93.8 \\
1266	Mediation & $+$56.7 & $+$70.4 & $+$77.2 & $+$85.3 & $+$88.7 \\
1267	IV & $+$1.4 & $+$22.1 & $+$50.1 & $+$66.7 & $+$76.4 \\
1268	M-bias & $-$125.3 & $-$43.6 & $-$19.6 & $+$6.0 & $+$18.5 \\
1269	\bottomrule
1270	\end{tabular}}
1271	\end{table}
1272	
1273	For confounding and exposure, PPI++ achieves $>$75\% RMSE reduction even at 5\% gold labels; IV requires ${\geq}20\%$ gold to reach 50\% reduction, reflecting the Wald estimator's higher variance. M-bias exhibits harmful correction at ${\leq}20\%$ gold ($-125\%$ at 5\%), because PPI++ re-exposes the M-bias pathway that annotation error had attenuated; only at ${\geq}50\%$ gold does the estimator become weakly beneficial ($+6\%$).
1274	
1275	\subsection{Realistic Differential Misclassification Thresholds}
1276	\label{sec:realistic_epsilon}
1277	
1278	Our non-differential assumption (Definition~\ref{def:nondiff}) requires $P(A^*{\mid}A,Y) = P(A^*{\mid}A)$. We estimate how much $Y$-stratified differential error each of our 23 empirical LLM confusion matrices can tolerate before exposure sign reversal exceeds 5\%. For each matrix $C$, we construct $C_{\text{high}} = \text{renorm}(C + \varepsilon\Delta)$ and $C_{\text{low}} = \text{renorm}(C - \varepsilon\Delta)$ with random off-diagonal perturbation $\Delta$ (500 MC trials per $\varepsilon$), sweep $\varepsilon \in [0, 0.50]$, and report the safe threshold $\varepsilon^*_{\text{safe}} = \max\{\varepsilon : \text{reversal rate} < 5\%\}$ (Table~\ref{tab:realistic_epsilon}).
1279	
1280	For $K{=}2$ (binary), no configuration produces sign reversal up to $\varepsilon{=}0.50$. For $K{\geq}3$, the majority of configurations are fragile ($\varepsilon^*_{\text{safe}}{=}0$), confirming that the non-differential assumption is load-bearing for multi-class exposure.
1281	
1282	\begin{table}[H]
1283	\centering
1284	\caption{Safe differential misclassification thresholds $\varepsilon^*_{\text{safe}}$ by dataset/$K$.}
1285	\label{tab:realistic_epsilon}
1286	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
1287	\toprule
1288	\textbf{Dataset} & $K$ & $n$ & \textbf{Mean} $\varepsilon^*$ & \textbf{Median} $\varepsilon^*$ & $\varepsilon^*{=}0$ \\
1289	\midrule
1290	Civil Comments & 2 & 7 & 0.50 & 0.50 & 0/7 \\
1291	VAST & 3 & 8 & 0.003 & 0.00 & 7/8 \\
1292	HuMAID & 10 & 8 & 0.045 & 0.00 & 7/8 \\
1293	\bottomrule
1294	\end{tabular}}
1295	\end{table}
1296	
1297	The distribution is sharply bimodal: all $K{=}2$ configurations are immune to differential misclassification within the tested range ($\varepsilon^*_{\text{safe}}{=}0.50$), consistent with the binary sign-preservation guarantee (Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip}(c)). For $K{\geq}3$, 14 of 16 configurations have $\varepsilon^*_{\text{safe}}{=}0$: sign reversal occurs at the smallest tested $\varepsilon{=}0.01$. Two outliers (VAST: $\varepsilon^*{=}0.02$; HuMAID: $\varepsilon^*{=}0.36$) have atypically structured confusion matrices. This confirms that even small violations of non-differential misclassification are consequential for multi-class exposure, underscoring the practical importance of validating $P(A^*{\mid}A,Y) \approx P(A^*{\mid}A)$ on gold-labeled subsets when $K \geq 3$.
1298	
1299	\subsection{Proportional Effects with Empirical Confusion Matrices}
1300	\label{sec:proportional_real_matrices}
1301	
1302	Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone} assumes proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$). We evaluate this assumption using all 23 empirical LLM confusion matrices under four DGP groups in the mediation topology (23 matrices $\times$ 50 seeds $\times$ 100{,}000 precomputed samples per seed).
1303	
1304	Evaluation uses the mediation DGP. $\lambda(C) \in [0,1]$: proportion satisfying the Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone} bound. Sign pres.: sign preservation rate of the direct effect estimate.

We estimate how much $Y$-stratified differential error each of our 23 empirical LLM confusion matrices can tolerate before exposure sign reversal exceeds 5\\%. For each matrix $C$, we construct $C_{\\text{high}} = \\text{renorm}(C + \\varepsilon\\Delta)$ and $C_{\\text{low}} = \\text{renorm}(C - \\varepsilon\\Delta)$ with random off-diagonal perturbation $\\Delta$ (500 MC trials per $\\varepsilon$), sweep $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.50]$, and report the safe threshold $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}} = \\max\\{\\varepsilon : \\text{reversal rate} < 5\\%\\}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:realistic_epsilon}).\n\nFor $K{=}2$ (binary), no configuration produces sign reversal up to $\\varepsilon{=}0.50$. For $K{\\geq}3$, the majority of configurations are fragile ($\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0$), confirming that the non-differential assumption is load-bearing for multi-class exposure.\n\n\\begin{table}[H]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Safe differential misclassification thresholds $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}$ by dataset/$K$.}\n\\label{tab:realistic_epsilon}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & $K$ & $n$ & \\textbf{Mean} $\\varepsilon^*$ & \\textbf{Median} $\\varepsilon^*$ & $\\varepsilon^*{=}0$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCivil Comments & 2 & 7 & 0.50 & 0.50 & 0/7 \\\\\nVAST & 3 & 8 & 0.003 & 0.00 & 7/8 \\\\\nHuMAID & 10 & 8 & 0.045 & 0.00 & 7/8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe distribution is sharply bimodal: all $K{=}2$ configurations are immune to differential misclassification within the tested range ($\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0.50$), consistent with the binary sign-preservation guarantee (Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}(c)). For $K{\\geq}3$, 14 of 16 configurations have $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0$: sign reversal occurs at the smallest tested $\\varepsilon{=}0.01$. Two outliers (VAST: $\\varepsilon^*{=}0.02$; HuMAID: $\\varepsilon^*{=}0.36$) have atypically structured confusion matrices. This confirms that even small violations of non-differential misclassification are consequential for multi-class exposure, underscoring the practical importance of validating $P(A^*{\\mid}A,Y) \\approx P(A^*{\\mid}A)$ on gold-labeled subsets when $K \\geq 3$.\n\n\\subsection{Proportional Effects with Empirical Confusion Matrices}\n\\label{sec:proportional_real_matrices}\n\nTheorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} assumes proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$). We evaluate this assumption using all 23 empirical LLM confusion matrices under four DGP groups in the mediation topology (23 matrices $\\times$ 50 seeds $\\times$ 100{,}000 precomputed samples per seed).\n\nEvaluation uses the mediation DGP. $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$: proportion satisfying the Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} bound. Sign pres.: sign preservation rate of the direct effect estimate.\n\n\\begin{table}[H]\n\\centering",

1324	
1325	\subsection{Case Study: ASV Diagnosis of Political Ad Tone Classification}
1326	\label{sec:asv_case_study}
1327	
1328	We illustrate the ASV diagnostic workflow on a concrete CSS application. \citet{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} use LLM classifiers to annotate political advertising tone ($K{=}3$: promote/contrast/attack) from the Fowler \& Ridout database, then estimate the causal effect of ad tone on voter outcomes. The annotated variable is the \textbf{exposure} (treatment). Using a representative $K{=}3$ confusion matrix from our empirical set (VAST DeepSeek zero-shot, mean diagonal 0.734) as a proxy:
1329	
1330	\begin{center}
1331	\small
1332	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{lccc}
1333	\toprule
1334	Coefficient & $\delta^*_{\text{mag}}$ (10\%) & $\delta^*_{\text{sf}}$ & Relative bias at $\delta{=}1$ \\
1335	\midrule
1336	$\beta_1$ (primary) & 0.234 & $> 1.5$ & 39.7\% \\
1337	$\beta_2$ (secondary) & 0.154 & $> 1.5$ & 60.4\% \\
1338	\bottomrule
1339	\end{tabular}}
1340	\end{center}
1341	
1342	Both coefficients are magnitude-unsafe ($\delta^* < 1$), with the secondary coefficient more fragile ($\delta^*{=}0.154$). If the same annotator were instead classifying a \textbf{confounder} (e.g., media framing as a control variable), magnitude ASV rises to $\delta^*{=}0.690$, a $3{\times}$ increase in error tolerance from the same confusion matrix. This demonstrates the central insight: topology, not annotator quality, determines vulnerability. Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction} routes this exposure topology to PPI++ or DSL correction; matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) are ineffective due to FWL non-applicability.
1343	
1344	\subsection{End-to-End Demonstration}
1345	\label{sec:e2e_demo}
1346	
1347	\input{e2e_demo_table.tex}
1348	
1349	The demonstration uses a symmetric $C_0$ ($d_{\mathrm{rel}}{=}0$); empirical $K{=}3$ matrices deviate substantially (median $d_{\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$, Remark~\ref{rem:symmetry}), but core ASV ranking and correction routing remain valid under Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}.
1350	
1351	\subsection{Semi-Real Case Study with Production LLM Confusion Matrices}
1352	\label{sec:real_cm_demo}
1353	
1354	We repeat the end-to-end pipeline using 10 confusion matrices estimated from production LLM annotation of VAST stance detection ($K{=}3$; DeepSeek, Llama4, Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3 under zero-shot and few-shot prompting). These matrices exhibit median $d_{\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$ (Remark~\ref{rem:symmetry}), far from the symmetric assumption of Theorem~\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}. Results confirm both topology-dependent patterns: PPI++ achieves 93.6\% median exposure bias reduction while plug-in corrections yield 0\% improvement for confounding (FWL invariance, machine precision; Table~\ref{tab:e2e-real-cm}).
1355	
1356	\input{e2e_real_cm_table.tex}
1357	
1358	\subsection{Correction Method Sensitivity to $K$}
1359	\label{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}
1360	
1361	We test whether the correction method ranking from Table~\ref{tab:correction} ($K{=}3$) is stable at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}10$. For each $K$, we sample 5 random Dirichlet confusion matrices (diagonal $\geq 0.6$), run 100 MC replications per topology--matrix combination ($N{=}5{,}000$, 10\% gold fraction), and compute RMSE reduction for all five methods across all seven topologies.
1362	
1363	\paragraph{Grand ranking stability.}

1440	Five topologies show the expected negative correlation: better annotation quality ($F_1{\uparrow}$) reduces causal bias. IV shows the strongest effect ($\rho = -0.70$), consistent with the Wald estimator's sensitivity to misclassification in the first stage. M-bias uniquely reverses: $\rho = +0.87$ ($p < 0.001$). The mechanism is that misclassification attenuates the spurious M-bias path, so the naive estimator under noisy annotations is closer to the true causal effect; improving annotation quality (higher $F_1$) removes this protective attenuation and increases bias. This is consistent with the correction results (\S\ref{sec:correction}), where PPI++ harms M-bias ($-$88\%) by removing the same protective noise. Collider shows a positive but non-significant trend ($\rho = +0.31$, $p = 0.15$), consistent with Berkson bias attenuation. Per-dataset breakdowns (CivilComments $K{=}2$: $\rho = -1.0$ for all topologies with $n{=}7$) should be interpreted with caution given the small sample and limited $F_1$ variation within the binary task.
1441	
1442	\subsection{Confusion Matrix Estimation Robustness}
1443	\label{sec:c_robustness}
1444	
1445	The ASV framework requires the confusion matrix $C$, which in practice must be estimated from a held-out gold-labeled subset. We assess how estimation noise in $\hat{C}$ propagates to the safe/fragile partition and ASV ranking. For each of 8 empirical confusion matrices (VAST, 4 LLMs $\times$ 2 prompting strategies), we draw $n_{\text{gold}} \in \{50, 100, 200, 500\}$ samples from a $\text{Multinomial}(n_{\text{gold}}, C_{\cdot k})$ distribution per column, compute $\hat{C}$, and evaluate the resulting ASV ranking across 200 bootstrap replicates.
1446	
1447	Spearman $\rho$ is computed vs.\ true-$C$ ranking under multinomial bootstrap from finite gold sets (8 empirical matrices $\times$ 200 bootstrap replicates per $n_{\text{gold}}$).
1448	
1449	\begin{table}[H]
1450	\centering
1451	\small
1452	\caption{$\hat{C}$ estimation robustness: partition stability and ASV ranking correlation.}
1453	\label{tab:c_robustness}
1454	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
1455	\toprule
1456	$n_{\text{gold}}$ & Partition stability & $\rho$ (median) & $\rho$ ($q_{25}$) \\
1457	\midrule
1458	50 & 50.4\% & 0.991 & 0.789 \\
1459	100 & 82.0\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\
1460	200 & 85.6\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\
1461	500 & 88.5\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\
1462	\bottomrule
1463	\end{tabular}
1464	\end{table}
1465	
1466	The ASV ranking is highly robust: median Spearman $\rho \geq 0.99$ even at $n_{\text{gold}}{=}50$. The safe/fragile partition (5 safe vs.\ 2 fragile topologies) recovers reliably from $n_{\text{gold}} \geq 100$ (82\% stability), increasing to 89\% at $n_{\text{gold}}{=}500$. The remaining instability concentrates in source matrices with low diagonal dominance (e.g., VAST DeepSeek few-shot: $\min C_{kk} = 0.16$), where bootstrap noise can shift borderline ASV values across the partition boundary. All 8 truth partitions match the canonical safe/fragile partition, confirming that the theoretical dichotomy holds empirically.
1467	
1468	\subsection{Sign Reversal Determinants}
1469	\label{sec:sign_determinants}

For each of 8 empirical confusion matrices (VAST, 4 LLMs $\\times$ 2 prompting strategies), we draw $n_{\\text{gold}} \\in \\{50, 100, 200, 500\\}$ samples from a $\\text{Multinomial}(n_{\\text{gold}}, C_{\\cdot k})$ distribution per column, compute $\\hat{C}$, and evaluate the resulting ASV ranking across 200 bootstrap replicates.\n\nSpearman $\\rho$ is computed vs.\\ true-$C$ ranking under multinomial bootstrap from finite gold sets (8 empirical matrices $\\times$ 200 bootstrap replicates per $n_{\\text{gold}}$).\n\n\\begin{table}[H]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{$\\hat{C}$ estimation robustness: partition stability and ASV ranking correlation.}\n\\label{tab:c_robustness}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n$n_{\\text{gold}}$ & Partition stability & $\\rho$ (median) & $\\rho$ ($q_{25}$) \\\\\n\\midrule\n50 & 50.4\\% & 0.991 & 0.789 \\\\\n100 & 82.0\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n200 & 85.6\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n500 & 88.5\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe ASV ranking is highly robust: median Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.99$ even at $n_{\\text{gold}}{=}50$. The safe/fragile partition (5 safe vs.\\ 2 fragile topologies) recovers reliably from $n_{\\text{gold}} \\geq 100$ (82\\% stability), increasing to 89\\% at $n_{\\text{gold}}{=}500$. The remaining instability concentrates in source matrices with low diagonal dominance (e.g., VAST DeepSeek few-shot: $\\min C_{kk} = 0.16$), where bootstrap noise can shift borderline ASV values across the partition boundary. All 8 truth partitions match the canonical safe/fragile partition, confirming that the theoretical dichotomy holds empirically.\n\n\\subsection{Sign Reversal Determinants}\n\\label{sec:sign_determinants}",

    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HvjAAXfqm1UKWRpTaQHSrY`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three natural language processing (NLP) tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	For each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$.
10	
11	\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}
12	\label{sec:taxonomy_results}
13	
14	\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}
15	Analytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\%--3.7\%), validating the closed-form derivations and justifying probability limits as practical bias diagnostics.
16	
17	\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}
18	Under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\% with 2.1\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\% (Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\% cause sign reversal; $\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\% accuracy (Appendix~\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\cite{brenner1993}. This establishes the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.
19	
20	\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}
21	Five topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\% amplification~\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).
22	
23	\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}
24	The IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.
25	
26	\begin{table}[t]
27	\centering
28	\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$. $\tau_{\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}
29	\label{tab:taxonomy}
30	\small
31	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
32	\toprule
33	\textbf{Topology} & \textbf{Violation} & \textbf{Sign Flip} & \textbf{Amplif.} & $\tau_{\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\
34	\midrule
35	Confounding & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
36	Exposure & 85\% & 83.4\% & 14.1\% & No \\
37	Mediation & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
38	Front-door & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
39	Collider & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
40	M-bias & 53.2\% & 0\% & 10.4\% & Yes \\
41	IV & 100\% & 52.6\% & 100\% & No \\
42	\bottomrule
43	\end{tabular}}
44	\end{table}
45	
46	Figure~\ref{fig:dirichlet_overlay} overlays all 23 empirical LLM configurations onto the theoretical violation-rate curves; real-world accuracy ($\min_k C_{kk} \geq 0.6$) places all models in the low-risk region for IV but leaves exposure vulnerable.
47	
48	\begin{figure}[t]
49	\centering
50	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig7_dirichlet_overlay.pdf}
51	\caption{Empirical LLM confusion matrices (markers) overlaid on theoretical violation-rate curves from $K{=}3$ Dirichlet analysis. Left: exposure; right: IV. Real-world accuracy places all models in the low-risk region for IV sign reversal but not for exposure.}
52	\label{fig:dirichlet_overlay}
53	\end{figure}
54	
55	\begin{figure}[t]
56	\centering
57	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}
58	\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\times}$ (IV 42.1\% vs.\ collider $<$5.6\%).}
59	\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}
60	\end{figure}
61	
62	\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}
63	\label{sec:asv_results}
64	
65	\begin{table}[t]
66	\centering
67	\small
68	\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}
69	\label{tab:asv_summary}
70	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
71	\toprule
72	\textbf{Topology} & \textbf{Mag ASV} & \textbf{Power} & \textbf{Sign-flip} \\
73	\midrule
74	IV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\
75	Front-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\% & --- \\
76	Confounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\% & --- \\
77	Mediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\%   & --- \\
78	Collider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\%   & --- \\
79	M-bias      & $\infty$   & 98\%  & --- \\
80	Exposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\% \\
81	\bottomrule
82	\end{tabular}
83	\end{table}
84	
85	\paragraph{ASV results.}
86	The ASV envelope is $\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic ($\varepsilon < 0.02$; Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}); IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\text{ASV} = \infty$; Figure~\ref{fig:asv_ranking}). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\% of exposure matrices yield $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs (Appendix~\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking, with the safe/fragile partition recovering from $n_{\text{gold}} \geq 100$ (82\% stability).
87	
88	\subsection{Empirical Validation}
89	\label{sec:empirical}
90	
91	We validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios. HumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\rho \leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.
92	
93	\subsection{Correction Guidance}
94	\label{sec:correction}
95	
96	\begin{figure}[t]
97	\centering
98	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_binarization.pdf}
99	\caption{Binarization collapse $\Delta$: additional bias from collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary. Topology determines severity ($>$150$\times$ range); exposure and IV exhibit sign flips.}
100	\label{fig:binarization}
101	\end{figure}
102	
103	We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}).
104	
105	\paragraph{FWL invariance.}
106	Under linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies (0\% bias reduction; Appendix~\ref{app:fwl_proof}): the treatment coefficient depends only on the projection orthogonal to the covariate space, so relabeling $A^* \to A$ leaves the coefficient unchanged. Under logistic regression this invariance breaks, but the method ranking persists ($|\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).
107	
108	\paragraph{Method ranking and decision procedure.}
109	PPI++ achieves the highest root mean squared error (RMSE) reduction (47.0\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\rho \geq 0.80$; Appendix~\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through risk assessment, method selection, and FWL screening. End-to-end demos (Table~\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\% to 0.2\%, while topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\%). Collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary is not a safe fallback: the additional bias $\Delta$ varies $>$150$\times$ by topology (Figure~\ref{fig:binarization}).
110	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three natural language processing (NLP) tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%), validating the closed-form derivations and justifying probability limits as practical bias diagnostics.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}. This establishes the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:dirichlet_overlay} overlays all 23 empirical LLM configurations onto the theoretical violation-rate curves; real-world accuracy ($\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.6$) places all models in the low-risk region for IV but leaves exposure vulnerable.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig7_dirichlet_overlay.pdf}\n\\caption{Empirical LLM confusion matrices (markers) overlaid on theoretical violation-rate curves from $K{=}3$ Dirichlet analysis. Left: exposure; right: IV. Real-world accuracy places all models in the low-risk region for IV sign reversal but not for exposure.}\n\\label{fig:dirichlet_overlay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$; Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking}). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs (Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv},~\\ref{sec:c_robustness}) confirm stable topology ranking, with the safe/fragile partition recovering from $n_{\\text{gold}} \\geq 100$ (82\\% stability; Appendix~\\ref{sec:asv_diagnostics},~\\ref{sec:asv_case_study}).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios. HumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_binarization.pdf}\n\\caption{Binarization collapse $\\Delta$: additional bias from collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary. Topology determines severity ($>$150$\\times$ range); exposure and IV exhibit sign flips.}\n\\label{fig:binarization}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies (0\\% bias reduction; Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}): the treatment coefficient depends only on the projection orthogonal to the covariate space, so relabeling $A^* \\to A$ leaves the coefficient unchanged. Under logistic regression this invariance breaks, but the method ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking and decision procedure.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest root mean squared error (RMSE) reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through risk assessment, method selection, and FWL screening. End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, while topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%). Collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary is not a safe fallback: the additional bias $\\Delta$ varies $>$150$\\times$ by topology (Figure~\\ref{fig:binarization}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three natural language processing (NLP) tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%), validating the closed-form derivations and justifying probability limits as practical bias diagnostics.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}. This establishes the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:dirichlet_overlay} overlays all 23 empirical LLM configurations onto the theoretical violation-rate curves; real-world accuracy ($\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.6$) places all models in the low-risk region for IV but leaves exposure vulnerable.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig7_dirichlet_overlay.pdf}\n\\caption{Empirical LLM confusion matrices (markers) overlaid on theoretical violation-rate curves from $K{=}3$ Dirichlet analysis. Left: exposure; right: IV. Real-world accuracy places all models in the low-risk region for IV sign reversal but not for exposure.}\n\\label{fig:dirichlet_overlay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$; Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking}). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs (Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv},~\\ref{sec:c_robustness}) confirm stable topology ranking, with the safe/fragile partition recovering from $n_{\\text{gold}} \\geq 100$ (82\\% stability; Appendix~\\ref{sec:asv_diagnostics},~\\ref{sec:asv_case_study}).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios. HumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_binarization.pdf}\n\\caption{Binarization collapse $\\Delta$: additional bias from collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary. Topology determines severity ($>$150$\\times$ range); exposure and IV exhibit sign flips.}\n\\label{fig:binarization}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies (0\\% bias reduction; Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}): the treatment coefficient depends only on the projection orthogonal to the covariate space, so relabeling $A^* \\to A$ leaves the coefficient unchanged. Under logistic regression this invariance breaks, but the method ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking and decision procedure.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest root mean squared error (RMSE) reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}) and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through risk assessment, method selection, and FWL screening. End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, while topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%). Collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary is not a safe fallback: the additional bias $\\Delta$ varies $>$150$\\times$ by topology (Figure~\\ref{fig:binarization}).\n",

Topology determines severity ($>$150$\\times$ range); exposure and IV exhibit sign flips.}\n\\label{fig:binarization}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies (0\\% bias reduction; Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}): the treatment coefficient depends only on the projection orthogonal to the covariate space, so relabeling $A^* \\to A$ leaves the coefficient unchanged. Under logistic regression this invariance breaks, but the method ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking and decision procedure.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest root mean squared error (RMSE) reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}) and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through risk assessment, method selection, and FWL screening. End-to-end demos (Appendix~\\ref{sec:e2e_demo},~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}; Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, while topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%). Collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary is not a safe fallback: the additional bias $\\Delta$ varies $>$150$\\times$ by topology (Figure~\\ref{fig:binarization}).\n",
